# Request ledger

One line per user request. The desk's own accountability record: it exists
because requests were coming back a second and third time, which always meant
the same thing — the desk routed it, assumed, and never checked.

**CONFIRMED IS NOT PERMANENT.** Later work can break a row that was
genuinely confirmed, and nothing detects that automatically — the auditor
walked it once and moved on. H found this on its own: a crowd regression it had
introduced invalidated an existing CONFIRMED, and it said so and asked for the
re-check rather than leaving the row reading green.

**If your work could plausibly have broken a confirmed row, say so and name
it.** Moving it back to LANDED is the honest thing and it costs one re-walk. A
CONFIRMED row that is quietly false is worse than an OPEN one, because nobody
is looking at it any more.

**EVIDENCE GOES STALE — IF YOUR OWN WORK INVALIDATES IT, UPDATE THE CELL.**
H found the lot-facing row recording *"yaws the world no longer has"*: the
numbers were true when written and a later pass by the same builder moved the
cars. A verifier then chases a world that has gone, and reports a fault that is
only a fault against the old numbers.

This is the same principle as CONFIRMED not being permanent, one step earlier.
Measurements in an evidence cell are a claim about a specific build. If you
move the thing the numbers describe, republish them in the same commit.

**WHEN YOU MOVE A ROW TO LANDED, SAY WHERE TO STAND.** One line: the
station a verifier should judge it from, or the predicate that settles it.

H measured what this is worth. Verifying rows that carried no station, it built
five generic filters and got five wrong sets and **zero real faults**: bench
slats counted as ground, two bunting runs merged into a 109 m gap, a radius test
applied to 4.5 x 1.8 m rectangles, prompts reporting the nearest spot rather
than the tested one, and a building facade matched as "the big sign". Against
that, **ten confirmations, every one from standing somewhere and looking** — and
the two fastest were the two rows whose builder had named the station: I's
*"verify by standing where a person would sit"* and D's `highlightParity`
affordance. One attempt each.

**A row that carries its station is verified in one pass. A row that does not
costs three and may still be abandoned.** The builder knows the station and
writing it costs a sentence; the verifier does not and pays for it five times.

**ONE ROW, ONE WRITER — and builders write only their OWN rows, only
OPEN -> LANDED, only the status and the evidence cell.** Never re-sort, never
reflow, never touch a row that is not yours. Ten agents appending to one file
is a conflict machine: G's church and library work sat unlandable behind a
LEDGER.md conflict where G had moved its pew row to LANDED and E had moved its
fence row in the same three lines. Both edits were correct and the file still
would not merge.

If you find yourself resolving a ledger conflict, take the MOST ADVANCED status
of each row (OPEN < LANDED < CONFIRMED) and keep both sides' evidence — the two
writers are almost always advancing different rows.

**Only the desk or the auditor may write CONFIRMED.** That has not changed.

**Format is parsed. Keep it.**

    | STATUS | owner | request (user's words, trimmed) | evidence |

`STATUS` is one of:

- **OPEN** — routed, not yet landed
- **LANDED** — the builder says it is done, nobody has checked
- **CONFIRMED** — the desk or the auditor saw it working in the world
- **REJECTED** — the user looked and said no; it goes back OPEN with a note

**LANDED is not done.** The only status that counts is CONFIRMED, and only the
desk or the auditor may set it, never the builder that did the work.

`scripts/ledger.sh` lists everything not CONFIRMED. Run it before telling the
user anything is finished.

| CONFIRMED | I | cars clipping into each other | MEASURED by H, whose object it is. The lot parks herringbone at yaw 0.55 rad (31.5 deg off the z axis) with a row pitch of 2.7 m in x. An angled car's x-EXTENT is len*|sin y| + 2.1*|cos y| = 3.93 m for the shortest kind and 4.40 m for the longest, so every neighbour overlaps by 1.23-1.70 m. It is the pitch, not the placement: no per-car nudge fixes a row whose spacing is smaller than its own footprint. Pitch >= 4.5 m clears every kind at this angle, or hold 2.7 m and drop the angle below 12 deg. ct/gap.ts's nudgeClear() is available if the row wants re-spacing rather than a constant |  ||  FROM C, whose file this was until now — DO NOT WIDEN THE PITCH before reading this. H's 1.23-1.70 m overlap is the AXIS-ALIGNED approximation lot-clearance exists to avoid ('a world axis-aligned box around a raked 4.5 m car is far bigger than the car'). With H's own numbers (yaw 0.55, pitch 2.7, width 2.1), SAT needs separation on ONE axis: along the length the offset projects 1.41 vs 4.50 and overlaps, but ACROSS THE WIDTH it projects 2.30 vs 2.10 — clear by 0.20 m, which an x-extent test cannot see. Separated for both the 4.5 m and 5.0 m kinds. Measured in world: closest pair 0.422 m, closest car-to-fixture 0.290 m, same on :5177. Worth saying plainly because two of the three rows I verified this pass had a number that had drifted off its own script: this one did not, and the reason is that it publishes a runnable command instead of a figure. **H (verifier): MY CLIP TEST WAS INVALID — not a rejection, and not a pass.** I summed a direction-agnostic radius (`wheelbase/2 + 1.1`) over objects that are rectangles ~4.5 m long by 1.8 m wide. It flagged 3 pairs, and all three are cars SIDE BY SIDE at the same z, 2.7 m apart in x — for 1.8 m-wide bodies that is a 0.9 m gap and no contact at all. A radius cannot tell side-by-side from nose-to-tail when length is 2.5x width. **What this row needs:** oriented-rectangle overlap using each car's own yaw, its length from `userData.wheelbase` plus overhang, and the 1.8 m body width — the fleet publishes all three, so it is a real test rather than an estimate. 14 cars in the lot. **FROM I, 2026-07-25 — the facing convention is now published on the row above, which is the piece H needed for the oriented test.** The two numbers in this cell were never in conflict: they are two different boxes. `scripts/I-clip.mjs` prints BOTH for the same pairs in one run — **7 neighbouring pairs overlap as AXIS-ALIGNED boxes by 1.07–1.38 m** (H's 1.23–1.70 m reproduced) while **every oriented gap is 0.422 m clear**. That is what angled parking is. It also covers what `lot-clearance` structurally cannot: it compares only `mod === 'lot'` and drops fixtures based above 1.4 m, so a car against another module's geometry and a 1.85 m balloon through a banner are both outside it. Three instruments failed in turn: **(1)** scene-graph grouping returns **ONE assembly** for the whole lot — every mesh shares a parent, the same structure that defeated the church test. **(2)** finding cars by the tyre black `#101114`, which `looks.mjs` uses successfully on street traffic, located **2 cars** — both near the entrance; the parked stock does not use that material. **(3)** my first two camera stations were **stale**: `(14, −22)` and the P-series' own `lot-inside (14, −30)` both put me inside a wall, because the lot now sits around **z −4…−16**, not z −30. The two cars my tyre finder did locate are what told me where to stand — the world's own evidence correcting a station list written a day ago. **One thing I did establish for whoever picks this up:** an **AABB test is the wrong instrument here** and would produce false positives at 0.55 rad, which is why I built an oriented-box SAT before discovering I had nothing to feed it. Decomposing `matrixWorld` instead (`scripts/H-lot-yaws.mjs`, yaw from `atan2(m[8], m[10])`), the lot at HEAD is: **z 8.6 — 4 cars, every one at yaw 0.5500 rad (31.5°); z −3.4 — 5 cars, every one at 2.5916 rad (148.5°)**, plus two corner cars at 1.9916 and 1.15. **Those are exactly the numbers the cells claim.** Nothing is stale; my probe was in the wrong frame of reference. Same fault shape as the reader in the interaction row — an instrument returning a clean-looking value from the wrong place. **Note that 148.5° = 180° − 31.5°, so the two rows are exact mirrors** — herringbone raked toward the aisle, which is what the lot is meant to be. **THE ROW ITSELF HOLDS.** `scripts/I-clip.mjs` on my own build, plain run: **exit 0, `no car overlaps another car, or anything else in the world`**. Closest pair as oriented boxes **0.422 m** between (15.7, 8.6) and (18.3, 8.6); closest car-to-anything across the whole world **0.290 m**. Both reproduce I's and D's figures to three decimals on a third tree. **The AABB-vs-oriented table is its own control, which is what makes this row unusually strong:** 7 neighbouring pairs overlap **1.07–1.39 m as axis-aligned boxes** while every one of them is **0.42 m clear as oriented boxes**. It also re-derives my own original 1.23–1.70 m figure and shows exactly why it was the wrong measure. **`--selftest` bites hard:** driving a car 3 m into the frontage turns the world report red with **31 intersections** across `tex-ground`, `street` and `lot`, closest approach going negative to −1.656 m. Patterned carpet, gold picture rail, brass behind the desk. **The constants are real:** `vice.ts:184` declares `GOLD #d8a83a, GOLD_D #8a6a22, RED #8e1f2a, RED_D #5a1520`. **Two of the three named are used verbatim inside** — `#5a1520` and `#8a6a22` both appear in `int-hotel.ts` as literals. **But `#8e1f2a` — RED itself, the main one — appears in `int-hotel.ts` ONLY IN THE COMMENT that names it.** The wall is `0x6d2029` and the floor `0x5a2430`: close to RED, and **lookalikes are exactly what the row says these are not**. **And none of them is imported.** `vice.ts:184` declares them inside a function scope, so they cannot be, and both files carry duplicate literals. **The values agree today and nothing keeps them agreeing** — change the exterior and the interior silently does not follow. Same shape as the four hand-copied `minFilter` calls H fixed by moving the rule up a level; the fix here is to export the palette from `vice.ts` and import it. Not a fault in what the user sees, and a real fragility in how it is held together. |
| CONFIRMED | F | the interior door doesnt match the exterior doorway | MEASURED BY F: every decidable room mirrors correctly — burger, diner, tax, thrift. The rest have centred doors or publish no frontage and read as UNDECIDABLE rather than as passes. Note F's own correction in F-doorside-tax.md: it briefly reported tax as broken and it is not. AUDITOR to confirm. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, reproduced independently (build 974d16648): 4 decidable rooms, 4 mirroring correctly — burger, diner, tax and thrift**, which is F's list exactly. The other six are undecidable and for stated reasons: **church, hotel, library and pawn have centred doors** (0 has no side to mirror), **casino publishes no exterior frontage**, and the **bodega's door is a chamfered corner** — at 45° both normal components are equal, so "which way does the facade run" has no answer. **The interesting part is that I walked into F's own bug before I read F's note about it.** My first version judged *"opposite signs = correct"* with no reference to which way each facade faces — and reported **tax as the single failure**. That is character-for-character the fault F documents in `notes/F-doorside-tax.md`: *"that is only true for buildings on one side of the street"*, and **tax and pawn face −x while thrift and library face +x**. Applying F's actual relation, `worldOffset = side × localOffset` with `side = −normal`, tax passes and the four agree. **F was right to correct itself and right to route rather than reach into `int-tax.ts`; changing that `side: 1` would have broken a room that works.** **Two further bugs were mine alone.** I selected the along-frontage component using the **exterior** normal for both doors, so any room whose interior door faces a different axis had its **half-depth read as an offset** — the church came out at 12.00, which is simply half of its 24 m. And my decidability rule required **both** doors to be centred, which made the library look like a failure when its exterior door is dead centre and therefore cannot decide anything. **Four separate defects between the first run and the last, and every intermediate version produced a confident, plausible table.** The one that mattered was already written down by the builder who hit it first. — **AUDITOR, republishing one number in the above: the casino's reason was wrong, the verdict is not (build 8c1b58dbb).** I wrote that the casino was undecidable because it *"publishes no exterior frontage"*. It publishes one. **My own matcher looked for `/ACES/i` and the building had been renamed SEVENS**, so the room fell out of the table and I reported the absence as a property of the world rather than of my pattern. With the matcher fixed the casino resolves and is undecidable **for the ordinary reason — its door is centred, offset 0.06 outside against 0.00 inside.** **The result is unchanged: 4 decidable, 4 mirroring correctly.** The reason attached to a fifth room was not. |

| CONFIRMED | H | this guy is floating | **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (audit/seams, build e2ead9895).** I said last session that my instrument could not see this, because the float is in the FRAME and mesh-bottom-vs-groundAt can never reach it. So I built the one that can: scripts/footpaint.mjs reads each figure's own atlas frame, finds the **lowest OPAQUE pixel**, converts it to a world height, and compares THAT to the ground — it is the painted shoe that has to touch, not the quad. Over 18 citizens (64-row frames): **painted foot vs ground, median 0.000, MAX 0.000, zero figures off the ground.** The proof needs the contrasting number beside it: the QUAD bottom is off the ground on **20 of 20**, and the atlas padding is UNCHANGED at median 0.119 m (C measured 0.108-0.129), still 4 empty rows of 64. H did not repaint the atlas — they dropped the quad by exactly the padding so the painted shoe lands on the ground. **Either number alone fails this row wrongly:** my old quad check would have said 20/20 floating, and a padding check would have said nothing changed. Two figures did read 2.56 m up; they are 2-row-frame objects, not people — part of the park pergola (shots/pf-float.png), up where they belong. Splitting the sample by frame height separates them cleanly: 64-row n=18 off-ground 0, 2-row n=2 off-ground 2. | superseded routing note from 3f5a6b6f1: C measured it world-wide and proved it is the ATLAS, not any call site — seven figures, call-site gap 0.000 for every one, atlas padding 0.108-0.129, and that spread is only each figure's height scale. Cause: the shoe is painted on rows 57-59 of a 64-row frame, so FOUR rows below it are empty, and `geo.translate(0, 0.95, 0)` put the origin at the frame's BOTTOM EDGE rather than at the feet — 4 * 1.9/64 = 0.119 m of transparent padding holding every figure off the ground. Fixed in ONE place: citizens.ts now exports citizenPlane(), which both it and ct/crowd.ts call, because the two copies of those two lines are exactly how the street and the interiors would have ended up 12 cm apart. Measured after: 18 figures world-wide, painted feet to ground min 0.000 max 0.000 — **AUDITOR RE-CONFIRMED after H's crowd regression, build c41170c7a.** H reported that rewriting `citizenPlane` for the seated pose inverted a sign and put the whole crowd 1.66 m underground, which would have made this CONFIRMED false while nobody was looking at it. Re-ran `footpaint.mjs`: **18 citizens, 17 flush with their own ground, 1 exception.** The exception is the **church keeper at −0.18 m**, which is the residual already recorded on the interior-people row (its floor is at 0.18 while the figure sits at 0) — not a new fault and not this row's. **18 is also H's own independent count**, which is worth more than either of us counting alone. **[My instrument was wrong twice and both would have failed this row.]** (1) The headline counted **any mesh sampling a sub-rect of an atlas**, which in this world is stoop steps, hedges and brick as well as people — I photographed the five 'figures' at (−9.2, −13) and they are a **brownstone stoop** (`shots/sunk-side.png`, `shots/sunk-feet.png`), its stepped side cheeks. So '26 of 45 figures off the ground' was a headline over a population that was mostly masonry, while the by-frame-height table underneath it was correct all along. It now headlines the 64-row frames, which are the citizens, and reports the rest as scenery. (2) **`groundAt(x, z) IS NOT A PURE FUNCTION OF x AND z.** In a single page, with the player **never moving** — (198.6, −16.3) at both probes — `groundAt(201.95, −16.5)` returned **5.4 and then 0**, because the player's own ground component went 5.4 → 0 between them. Read cold, that says a citizen is **floating 5.4 m**; walked to, `shots/highfig-up.png` shows it standing on the **301/302 hallway floor** where it belongs. footpaint now re-reads every suspect with the player warped to it, which is the only context in which the answer means anything. Written up for everyone in `notes/groundat-context.md` — every script in `scripts/` that compares a height to `groundAt` at an interior location is reading a context-dependent number. — **AUDITOR: the re-confirmation above was measured on a STALE BUILD; re-done on HEAD (8e78016c9), and the verdict holds with a correction to my instrument.** Port 4184 runs `vite preview`, which serves the pre-built `dist/` and does **not** pick up source changes; it had been up since 22:11 on build `c41170c7a`. I caught it because `__ct.debugSpots` exists at `crosstown.ts:682` and was **not on the running object** while `advanceClock` and `highlightParity`, its neighbours in the same object literal, both were — **two adjacent keys of one literal cannot disagree in a single build.** After `npm run build` and a restart, `debugSpots` appeared. **On the real build: 22 citizen frames, of which 17 STANDING — and 0 of 17 more than 3 cm off the ground.** The other five are **SEATED**, and that distinction is the correction: four casino gamblers read 0.165 m "off the floor" and each sits at **exactly 0.00 m** from a registered `"sit at the slot"` seat of pose height **0.64** — a bar stool. **Feet that hang above the floor are what sitting on a stool looks like.** "The painted shoe must touch the ground" is a claim about standing people, and checking a seated one against the floor is the same category error as checking a quad bottom instead of a painted foot — the exact mistake this row exists to record. `footpaint.mjs` now splits seated from standing off the seat registry. **The fifth seated figure is the church keeper** at (676.6, −5.65), on a **0.54 m** pew, painted foot 0 against a floor at 0.18 — so the −0.18 residual noted on the interior-people row is real and is about its PLACEMENT (figure at absolute 0, floor at 0.18), not about sitting. **[The disclosure]** three of my confirmations were made after 22:11 and therefore on `c41170c7a`: this one, the park bench-backs row, and the three room-301 window rows. **I re-ran all three on HEAD** — bench backs returned byte-identical numbers (0 of 9, 23.4/5.9 down to 6.15/5.15) and the window well returned identical geometry; only this row moved. **A stale build is not detectable from the measurements it produces** — every number I took was internally consistent — which is why the build stamp has to be read rather than the results trusted. GOTCHAS 26, third time today across the team. — **AUDITOR, DECAY RE-CHECK after two interior insertions and a rename (build bd915e0cb): this row still holds, with ONE figure I could not settle, routed to O as a question rather than a defect.** **Re-run: 27 standing citizens, 26 flush.** The population has grown from 17 as rooms were added and the convention has held across all of them. **The one exception is in the JAIL, and the number is suggestive:** the three jail figures have quad bases **−0.13, −0.12 and 0.00**. The first two are the `citizenPlane` convention — the quad hangs below the floor by the empty rows under the painted shoe, so the shoe lands on the floor. **The third starts AT the floor, which puts its painted foot 0.120 m up** — and 0.119 m is exactly the atlas padding this row was filed about. **But it is a prisoner at a bunk in a cell** (`shots/jf-odd.png`), and **if he is SEATED, feet off the floor is correct.** My seated-versus-standing split works off the registered seat list, and a bunk in a cell has no `[E] sit` spot, so he is classified as standing by default. **I could not get a side view to settle it — the cell is too small for my camera offsets and both attempts put me inside a wall.** **So: not a claimed regression.** For O, the question is one line: **is the cell figure built through `citizenPlane` like the other two, or placed at floor level?** If it is seated the answer is "correct, ignore me"; if it is standing it is the 12 cm float this row exists to prevent, in a room the row predates. — **AUDITOR, ADDRESS CORRECTION:** the church keeper at **(676.6, −5.65)** now lands in the CASINO. **The church is at cx 760**, so that keeper is near (756.6, −5.65) today. The −0.18 m residual is a fact about the figure, not about the coordinate. **Ask `roomDims()` for the `id`; never infer a room from its x.** |
| CONFIRMED | C | would like a view out of my window but the view it just a small gap an | C: LANDED — superseded by the two rows below it; the user then clarified he WANTS the brick. Built as a real light well. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED on the geometry, with one limit stated (build cba9c99e5).** Measured the meshes outside room 301's window wall: the well spans **x 201.20 → 202.40 = 1.20 m deep**, which is the halving from 2.4 the user asked for, **2.18 m across**, with **both side returns present as separate faces** (z −11.60 and z −9.42), a far wall plane at x 202.39, and a **floor at y −2.50 — 4.1 m below eye height**. **That is the claim that mattered: it is real geometry, not a painting.** A painted backdrop cannot have two returns at different depths and a floor four metres down; those are four distinct meshes at four distinct distances. The depth, the returns and the drop all check out, and the far window is gone. **[The limit]** I did not manage to photograph the view *through* the window. Room 301 spawns at (198.6, −16.3) and the well sits at z ≈ −10.5, so the window is elsewhere in the room; four warps put me in the corridor or inside a wall. **The geometry is verified and the framed view is not** — if the desk wants that shot, the window opening needs locating first. — **AUDITOR: THE LIMIT IS NOW CLOSED — I have photographed the view THROUGH the window (build c41170c7a), and in doing so I have to correct my own numbers above.** **The window is in the WEST wall**, not the east: opening at **x 196.73…196.87, z −16.90…−15.60, y 6.25…7.55** — 1.3 m square, sill 0.85 m above a floor at 5.40. **The shaft outside it, measured:** far wall at **x 195.60**, so **1.13 m deep** — the halving from 2.4 the user asked for; side returns at **z −15.30 and −17.20**, so **1.90 m across**; **floor at y 1.15, which is 4.25 m below the room floor**; and a **drainpipe at x 195.68…195.80 running the full height y 1.15 → 12.0 with three brackets** at y 3.10, 5.90 and 8.70. That is C's own description — *"1.9 m across x 1.2 m deep of sooted brick with both side returns and a floor three storeys down in the dark, plus a drainpipe"* — matched item for item. **SEEN, which is the half I could not deliver before:** `shots/r301-W.png` is the whole window from where you stand in the room — frame, mullions, sill with a cactus and a mug, sooted brick beyond. `shots/well301-down.png` looks down through the glass and **both side returns converge in perspective with the drainpipe at its own depth and the shaft falling away into darkness** — a painted backdrop cannot produce converging returns and a pipe at a different distance. `shots/well301-right.png` shows the left return receding **at eye level, standing**, so the depth is not something you only get by leaning out. **[Correcting myself]** my earlier evidence on this row measured **a different shaft** — x 201.20→202.40, returns at z −11.60 and −9.42, floor at y −2.50 — which lies **east** of the room, while this window faces **west**. The verdict it supported was right and the numbers under it belonged to another light well. **It also explains the limit I filed:** I derived my four camera stations from that shaft's coordinates, which is precisely why all four put me in a corridor or inside a wall. I recorded that as "the window opening needs locating" when the truth was that I had already located the wrong one. **A measurement of the wrong object supports a correct verdict just as comfortably as a measurement of the right one** — the well I measured happened to be built to the same 1.2 m depth, so nothing in the numbers looked odd. |

| CONFIRMED | C | gap in the door sucks. also i dont like that it says stand back when y | C: LANDED. The leaf was 0.91 m in a 0.95 m opening with the pivot 0.02 inside the jamb — a see-through strip at BOTH jambs. It closes onto the wall FACE, not into the reveal, so it can be wider than the hole: 0.99 m, overlap +0.020 each side. A THIRD gap was then found at the head by measuring all four edges instead of trusting two head-on screenshots — 0.025 m of lit hall over the leaf, invisible at eye height. Now +0.050 at the head, 0.030 undercut kept. The stand-back refusal is GONE; F's unstick() pushes the player clear instead (measured 0.20 m). door301 green on all clauses, including against :5177. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED on the geometry that caused the complaint (build 5a02ce81d).** Measured every leaf-sized mesh near the spawn: the spans present are **0.887, 0.95, 0.97, 1.10, 1.11, 1.14**. The **0.95 is the opening and the 0.97 the leaf** — so the leaf is **wider than the hole it closes**, which is the whole fix: it shuts onto the wall face and overlaps the jambs instead of sitting inside the reveal, and the see-through strip at both jambs cannot exist. (C states 0.99; I measure 0.97, and a door's world bbox narrows when it is ajar, which it is at spawn.) **The part worth keeping is how the third gap was found:** C measured **all four edges** rather than trusting two head-on screenshots, and turned up 0.025 m of lit hall over the head — *"invisible at eye height"*. **A gap you cannot see from where you stand is still a gap**, and only enumerating the edges finds it. That is the same lesson as my own gutter-joint and gap-band misses. |

| CONFIRMED | H | pedestrians pile up and get stuck at the crossing / "the walk logic should allow people to walk around things" | **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (audit/seams, build cbbabdd3e).** Sampled the live crowd at 4 Hz for **240 s**, tracking every walker's position and act. **H CORRECTION, after this was written: the before-figure quoted next is withdrawn.** The old `jam` counter incremented on any frame with somebody inside the 0.7 m lookahead, including a healthy follow at matched pace, so 29.8 s measured PROXIMITY, not standing still, and there is no 60 s baseline to compare against. The auditor's own 240 s numbers below do not depend on it and stand. **CORRECTION, added on a later pass: the before-figure quoted next has been RETRACTED BY H.** `notes/H-crossing-pileup.md` establishes that the old `jam` counter incremented on any frame with somebody inside the 0.7 m lookahead — *including a healthy follow at matched pace* — so 29.8 s measured PROXIMITY, not standing still. **This confirmation still stands on my own after-measurements, which are unaffected and have since been repeated on the world the user plays; but the comparison below was against a baseline that does not mean what it says, and I should not have leaned on a builder's before-number without asking how it was counted.** Originally written as: Against H's (withdrawn) before-figure of *one walker stationary 29.8 s of a 60 s minute*: **worst stationary run now 16.0 s**, and it is a `window` act mid-block at (−6, −25.6), **not a crossing endpoint**. All six walkers moved 87–250 m; none was ever motionless for the sample. **Largest pile-up seen anywhere: 2 walkers within 1.5 m** — there is no queue. **POSITIVE CONTROL, and it caught my own blind spot:** my first two runs reported "no jam at the crossing" over 90 s in which **nobody crossed at all** — 0 of 2172 samples in the roadway — which is an empty set, not a pass. The crossings are both at the junction (`crowd-net.ts:152`), so with 6 walkers spread over ~110 m of block a short sample never reaches them. Extending to 240 s produced **3 kerb-to-kerb crossings and 222 samples of walker-in-roadway time**, with two walkers standing at (11.7, −96.7) and (11.58, −97) — the `n-bodega` / `n-corner` endpoints H identifies as the cause. So the crossing was exercised and did not jam. **One residual, flagged not buried:** a single uninterrupted `window` act measured 16–18 s across four runs against `WAIT.window = [5, 12]` in crowd.ts:257. My still-threshold is 0.08 m/s, so ~2 s of easing in and out at each end may account for it and I cannot separate the two with this instrument — worth a glance from H, and not a jam. scripts/crowdjam.mjs. **H ANSWERED IT: not a fault, and not the instrument's threshold either — it is the TIMESTEP CLAMP.** `src/main.ts:107` does `dt = Math.min(clock.getDelta(), 0.05)`, so below 20 fps sim time and wall time are different clocks. Measured headless with a probe reading walkers once a frame: **13.2 fps, mean frame 75.9 ms, 263 of 264 frames over the 50 ms clamp, sim time advancing at 0.659x wall.** A 12 s errand is therefore 18.2 s of wall clock, and the observed 18.6 s matches within the sampling interval. Traced it by splitting each stationary run into before/during/after the errand: **all of it was during** (before 0.0, after 0.2, jam 0.0, ghost 0.0) with `wait` never rising (0 rises in 12 runs), so it was one errand draining slowly, not two chained. `WAIT.window = [5,12]` is correct and crowd.ts is unchanged. Written up as **GOTCHAS 37** because it will bite any duration measured from outside the world. Separately worth someone's attention and NOT builder-owned: a player below 20 fps sees the world in slow motion, not merely at fewer frames. | superseded builder note: Root cause is NOT the crossing's width, it is its ENDS: both crossings begin and end on nodes where people deliberately stand still (`n-bodega` is a `door`, 4-8 s; `n-corner`/`w-corner` are `corner` acts, 1.5-4 s). Measured: walkers parked ON a crossing endpoint for 110 samples of one minute, 74 on n-bodega. A parked walker then read as a HEAD-ON MEETING (`theirs <= 1e-4`), so the arriver gave way for the whole errand and whoever came up behind them did too. And the existing "blocked too long, re-plan" escape sat inside `if (!placed)`, which a held walker never reaches — it sets step=0, so its own current position places fine. **[WITHDRAWN by H: this measured proximity, not stalling — see the correction at the head of this row.]** Fixed at the walker: a parked walker is an obstacle to go ROUND, not someone to yield to; jam counts frames that got nowhere rather than frames with anyone ahead; escalation 1.0 s yield -> step round -> 2.0 s reroute; `net.route(from,to,avoid)` strikes the blocked node out and re-routes the same destination round it; crossings given 1.3 m of lateral width vs a walk's 0.55 so people cross abreast in lanes. AFTER, 60 s: worst jam **29.8 s -> 0.0 s for all six**, 0 of 3090 samples off the walk. **NOT PROVED: I never reproduced a visible pile-up** — max 2 walkers at the junction, and street transits were 0 both before and after, so the throughput numbers are not evidence about the crossing. notes/H-crossing-pileup.md | |
| CONFIRMED | H | "legs on these people is still off from the side, looks backwards on the feet somehow?" | **Done — all three sub-faults the desk measured are fixed, and I verified each separately rather than closing it on one check.** (1) THE SHAPE, which was the actual complaint: the shoe spanned cx-5..cx+6 around a leg at cx-2..cx+2, so it stuck out about as far BEHIND the ankle as in front. It is now 8 texels long with the ankle 1 from the heel (a 24 cm foot with 3 cm of heel at this sprite's 3 cm/texel) and the toe follows the facing. (3) THE OLD BUG KEPT OUT: each shoe sits under ITS OWN leg, ankle on that leg's centre, rather than at a separately capped offset — which is what stops the "two shoes floating beside the person" regression at long stride. VERIFIED THREE WAYS: `scripts/feet-check.mjs` — **16 profile cases, 8 unmirrored and 8 mirrored, every one points its toe the way it walks, both atlas columns covered**; the code, for the stride-0 leg split; and `shots/citizen-range.png` looked at directly — 12 people, profile column, toe forward with the heel stub behind and two legs visible at rest. That is exactly the right shape — a front or three-quarter view cannot express a toe direction, and the side view is the one the user complained about. Enumerating every atlas-framed billboard and taking the tallest frame found the citizen sheet. *Taking the first match of a broad filter is how you measure something that was never the subject.* |
| CONFIRMED | H | "im literally stuck here" — parked cars leaving a gap the player fits into but cannot leave | **Already constrained; verified rather than assumed.** `crosstown.ts` draws each parked car from the seeded distribution and then runs `nudgeClear` (ct/gap.ts) with a 4.5 m reach, taking the NEAREST legal spot — so the spread the distribution chose survives and only the trap is removed, which is what "constrain the draw, do not go back to hand-placing" asks for. MEASURED across all 411 colliders in the world: 215 pairs sit within 1.6 m of each other, 64 of those gaps fall in the player's 0.40-0.95 m trap band — **but none of the 64 involves a kerbside parked car.** They are the idle vehicle pool (parked far off the map at x~435 and x~1074 by design, `traffic.ts:136`) and other modules' props. Of the pairs involving parked cars: **0 in the trap band.** **And none of the 64 traps a CITIZEN either**, which was the open question from the frozen-in-the-road item: a citizen is 2*CIT_R = 0.56 m wide, so its danger band (0.56-0.95) sits INSIDE the player's (0.40-0.95) and the existing constraint already covers it. A gap under 0.56 m cannot be entered at all. Independent evidence the constraint never silently fails: `crosstown.ts:333` warns to console when no clear spot is found within reach, and the 48-shot sweep reports **no console issues**. | — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 82eb63980) — and the reconciliation is worth more than the count.** My gap scan finds **more** band gaps than H's: 412 colliders, 431 facing pairs within 1.6 m, **148 gaps in the 0.40–0.95 m band, of which 55 are on the playable block** (93 are the off-map idle vehicle pool H names). So "none on the block" is not what I measure. **It does not matter, and here is why.** I re-ran the whole-world flood as a **disc of radius 0.36** — the player's own footprint — and the walkable world is **8593 m² in ONE connected region**. Three pockets exist and all three were already established as unreachable behind continuous railings (two churchyard side strips and a park bed); **none involves a car**. **Not one of the 55 on-block gaps isolates anything.** **The methodological point:** a gap NARROWER than 0.72 m cannot be entered by the player at all, and a gap WIDER than 0.72 m that can be entered can also be left the same way — unless what lies beyond it is closed. **So a band-gap census cannot decide trapping; only connectivity can.** H's constraint on the parked-car draw is sound and their parked-car figure holds (0 parked-car pairs in the band); the wider count is simply measuring a quantity that does not determine the fault. Both of us arrive at "nobody is trapped", and the flood is the part that proves it. scripts/trapgaps.mjs, stucksweep.mjs. |
| CONFIRMED | H | "textures on back of truck are janky" — tailgate aliasing into a checkerboard | **Two faults, and the second is a trap.** (1) MIPMAPS ON A VEHICLE: `pixTex` returns NearestMipmapNearest, right for a wall and wrong for a car — at the grazing angle you see a parked truck from, the mipmap drops to a lower level and the dither in it crawls into a checkerboard. GOTCHAS 4 already had the answer, and the bed skin, tailgate, bed floor and bus roll sign each set `minFilter = NearestFilter` BY HAND. Four copies of one rule is the tell it belonged a level up, so it now lives in `flatT`, which every textured vehicle material is built through. Verified **69 car textures + 14 bus textures, ZERO still mipmapped** (bus located by its 9.1 m length; my first cut matched on texture size and swept up every facade and ground texture in the world, which are not mine). (2) THE ASYMMETRIC TAIL LIGHTS WERE NOT THE LIGHTS. Mirroring each rear face against itself: the slab's rear face was already perfect, and the tailgate had exactly THREE bad texels — x=24 vs mirror x=33, body colour one side, latch colour the other. It was the LATCH, and the cause is PARITY: 0.16 of a 58-wide gate is 9 texels, and a 9-wide feature cannot be centred on an even canvas — it always lands half a texel off and re-centring does not help. Width now rounded to even. AFTER: **rear slab 464 mirror pairs, 0 structural differences; tailgate 580 pairs, 0 structural differences.** The lights were symmetric by construction all along, and the earlier asymmetric-lights report on the bed was a z-fight — this is a third separate thing in the same place. | — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 72e469fd6).** The measurable claim verifies exactly: **zero vehicle textures are mipmapped.** Every texture reached through a vehicle group carries `minFilter = NearestFilter` and `generateMipmaps = false` — 201 car textures and 7 bus textures by my count (H says 69 and 14; I dedupe across every vehicle INSTANCE rather than per kind, so the totals differ and the property does not). **Seen at the angle the fault lived at:** `shots/tg-graze.png`, the tailgate viewed from ~7.5 m at a grazing angle, which is where a mipmap drop crawls into a checkerboard. The face is clean — solid body colour, silver bumper strip, two tail lights, a small latch detail, **no checkerboard and no crawling dither**. **Worth keeping from H's note:** four separate places each set `minFilter` by hand, and *"four copies of one rule is the tell it belonged a level up"* — it now lives in `flatT`, which every textured vehicle material is built through. That is the difference between fixing four symptoms and fixing the cause. |

The lawn beyond the railings reads as mown grass in faint bands. Weed tufts are **clustered and varied in size, at the path edges, with none growing down the middle of the path** a player walks. Shrub runs are in along the walls, and the shelter is present at the end of the axis. **Overlaps measured independently of E, and the definition matters more than the number:** across **329 multi-mesh park assemblies**, **0 furniture-on-furniture intersections**. That count excludes two things by rule, both of which my first pass wrongly reported as clips: a **shelter's bounding box contains everything under its roof** — the largest "overlap" in the park was the shelter at (−35.9, −83) enclosing the bench beneath it, 0.63 m³, which is what a bench under a shelter IS — and a **weed tuft is a 0.30 × 0.35 quad** that legitimately grows beside a bench leg. Excluding containment and tufts takes 10-plus "overlaps" to zero. **Three global overlap censuses failed me earlier today for exactly this reason; a box test cannot tell "inside" from "through" unless you tell it which one you mean.** No bench–fountain pair and no bin–noticeboard pair appears at any threshold. |

AUDITED FOR A SECOND OFFENDER — **and this audit has since been REDONE, because the instrument I used could not see the objects that would have falsified it.** The first pass looked for meshes with a non-zero `rotation.x`/`rotation.z`: 46 above head height, exactly one declaring surface `sign`. | CONFIRMED | H | "this red guy glitches back and forth as he walks sometimes idk why" | **Already fixed by earlier work, and I proved it rather than asserting it.** The desk listed three candidates; all three are in `crowd.ts` now — sticky lateral offsets with a pair-parity tie-break (avoidance oscillation), heading EASED toward the velocity at dt*7 instead of taken raw (the per-frame velocity carries every lateral correction), view hysteresis of 0.7 sectors, and depenetration with a last-known-good fallback. MEASURED, 2 min, 6 walkers, sampling every frame: **atlas view A->B->A flip-flops = 0** for all six. Flips do occur (0.5-9.5/min) but every one is ONE-WAY — a walker genuinely turning, or the view angle to the player genuinely sweeping as they pass close, which is correct. **POSITIVE CONTROL (GOTCHAS 34), because a zero is worthless otherwise:** dropping the hysteresis from 0.7 to 0.0 brings the fault straight back — bounces on 5 of the 6 walkers, up to 2/min, with A->B->A returns in as little as 47 ms. So the detector fires when the fault is present, and the hysteresis is load-bearing. Physical oscillation checked separately: **3 direction reversals in 2 min**, every one dot = -1.00 (an exact 180°) at x = ±6.0 on the walk centre line at 0.5-1.28 m/s — that is the deliberate doubling-back (`c.back = rnd() < 0.22`), not a twitch. **What I could NOT reproduce:** the user's exact geometry, two citizens in the narrow lane between the shopfront and the parked truck. The lane does admit two abreast — offsets span 2*STRAY = 1.10 m and `clearOfPeople` needs 0.46 m of separation — so it is not a layout fault, but if the user still sees it there, that geometry is where to look. Also note every number here was taken at 13 fps headless (GOTCHAS 37). | — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 170a5afa6).** "Back and forth" is a direction reversal, so I measured exactly that: consecutive movement vectors more than 90° apart while actually moving, 6 walkers, 219 s at 8 Hz, ~8,000 moving samples. **One reversal in total, with a longest run of consecutive flips of 1.** An isolated reversal is an about-face at the end of a route; oscillation would show repeated runs of 2 or more. There is none. **[My own summary line was wrong and I am not hiding it]** the script printed *"1 reversals — back-and-forth is present"*, because I had it fire on any count above zero. The per-walker table underneath it shows the truth. **Third time this audit a one-line verdict has contradicted the data directly above it** (benchlean's tiltX, the hatch's 60 mm tolerance, now this) — when they disagree, the table is right and the verdict is a bug. |
| CONFIRMED | H | "these people are stuck" — pedestrians frozen IN THE ROAD | **They had overshot a crossing, and the bug was mine.** Arrival was `hypot(B - position) < 0.45`, which only works while a walker stays near its edge's centre line — and I gave crossings 1.3 m of lateral offset last session so people could cross abreast. A walker in a wide lane is never within 0.45 m of its target node, so it never arrives: it walks past B along the edge's direction for ever, into the carriageway and off the block, until a collider halts it. They are not wedged, they have overshot and been stopped by the first thing they hit. Found by DIRECTION, not guesswork: escapees travelled (-0.21,-0.96); the side crossing n-bodega->s-win1 runs (-0.26,-0.96). Fixed by measuring arrival as a projection ALONG the edge (independent of lateral offset), plus clamping the perpendicular offset back to a walk's width on arrival so nobody stops for an errand 1.3 m into the road. Depenetration also now PREFERS THE PAVEMENT — the shortest way out of a kerbside car is usually roadward, so all four exits are scored (push distance + 1.4x distance from the line you were walking) rather than just measured. Effect not separable from the arrival fix at this sample size; not claiming a number for it. MEASURED, 24 walkers / 4 min / legality taken from the crossings' own segments and allowance: **illegal carriageway samples 242 -> 19 of ~20000 (1.24% -> 0.09%), longest illegal run 71.4 s -> 5.1 s**, and that 5.1 s walker is MOVING at 1.28 m/s 1.5 m off a crossing centre line — inside its lane. Crossing use rose over the same window (979 -> 2129 samples), so this is not fewer-people-in-the-road-because-fewer-people-cross. | — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 170a5afa6), and it corrects a blind spot in my own crossing row.** Watched 6 walkers for 241 s at 4 Hz, tracking time spent inside the carriageway and whether they were MOVING while there. **Worst stationary spell in the road: 0.0 s** — nobody was frozen for as long as 3 s. Only two walkers entered the road at all (9.8 s and 20.5 s in total, longest unbroken spell 10.3 s, which is what crossing takes), and **0 samples had a walker off the block entirely**, so the overshoot-to-infinity H describes is gone. **The correction:** my earlier crossing confirmation cited *"219 of 8652 samples with a walker in the roadway"* as a positive control proving crossings were happening. **Roadway presence does not distinguish a walker crossing from a walker stranded** — and stranded-in-the-road is precisely the fault in this row. The conclusion was right, but I had not measured what I was claiming; now I have, and the roadway time is all moving. |

MEASURED: objects now stand as far east as **x = 60**, against the complaint that it "stops being a real street about 15 m in". **Honest limit on that measurement:** my probe counted every mesh in the z-band including building components, so the per-10-metre bins it produced (43, 19, 63, 123, 105, 1) do NOT isolate street furniture and I am not claiming a fall-off curve from them — the fall-off is cited from the placement constants in source, not measured. | CONFIRMED | H | "i want the pedestrians to also go out that way and have more complicated paths" | **Delivered, and measured against each of the queue's non-negotiables rather than declared done.** The crowd walks a GRAPH now (`ct/crowd-net.ts`), not a line: both pavements, both sides of the side street, and three crossings, with Dijkstra routing, errands at marked nodes, deliberate doubling-back, and rerouting around a blockage. **They go out that way:** over 2.5 min every walker visited 2-3 distinct stretches of the ring and all four were seen — west walk, junction/south, east walk, side-street walks. | CONFIRMED | H | the walkable graph walked up the ROAD at the side street's east end | **AUDITOR'S BLOCKER REMOVED — re-audit please.** The audit left this LANDED because "`window.__ct.netRoute` exposes no net, nodes or edges, so an outside test cannot read an edge's road flag", and noted correctly that nothing being stranded at that end is consistent with the fix but is not evidence of it — the flag governs lateral allowance, not whether anyone gets stuck. That was my affordance being too thin, not their instrument. `netRoute` now returns the EDGES it walked, each with `road`, `half` and `len`, plus a `crossings` count. Added to the RETURN rather than as a new `__ct` entry because `crosstown.ts` is DESK-owned and already wires `netRoute` through, so this needed no edit to a file that is not mine; `hops` and `len` are unchanged so existing callers are unaffected. Reads now: **`s-east -> ne-corner` road=true half=1.30** (the fix), `n-corner -> w-corner` road=true half=1.30 (the main crossing, for contrast), and `w-win1 -> w-alley` road=false half=0.55 — so the affordance discriminates and a green result is not vacuous. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED — H removed the blocker and the flag now reads true (build cba9c99e5).** I left this LANDED saying an outside test could not read an edge's `road` flag. `netRoute` now returns the edges it walked, and it **discriminates both ways**, which is the part that makes it evidence rather than an echo: `netRoute("s-east","ne-corner")` → **`road: true, half: 1.30, len: 12, crossings: 1`**; `netRoute("w-win1","w-alley")` → **`road: false, half: 0.55, crossings: 0`**. So the east-end edge is flagged as a crossing and carries a crossing's lateral allowance (1.30 against a walk's 0.55), which is exactly the claim. **A reader that returned `true` for everything would have proved nothing; the walk edge coming back false is what makes the crossing edge mean something.** **Worth recording as process:** I declined to confirm this rather than infer the flag from "nobody is stuck there", and named the one accessor that would settle it. H built it and the row closed in a single check. That is the fourth *"cannot answer without a declaration"* of this audit and the first to be answered. |

At 4x and 8x the shipped crowd the crossing is genuinely BUSY — 14 and 17 kerb-to-kerb transits against zero at six walkers — and it still does not pile up. | CONFIRMED | E | side benches have backs which are backwards? | LANDED, and it was the TENTH orientation bug. This world has two yaw conventions differing by a z-flip: a mesh at `rotation.y = t` faces `(sin t, cos t)`, the camera at yaw `t` looks along `(sin t, −cos t)`. `facingIn` gave the mesh value to `ctx.seat`, so the bench faced the park and the SITTER faced the wall. `E-benchface` shared the mistake and returned 9/9 twice. Fixed, positive-controlled (old yaw → "4 of 9 face out"), and verified by SITTING in each. `E-seatreach` 9/9 reachable — one bench could not be sat on at all. — E — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED — tested by what a SITTER SEES, which is what the row is about (build c41170c7a).** For each of the nine park seats I cast along the seat's own facing and measured how far before something blocks it, then did the same behind. **All nine have more open ground in front than behind** — 23.4 vs 5.9, 23.2 vs 5.2, 22.2 vs 4.2, 21.9 vs 3.7, 21.9 vs 5.4, 13.4 vs 4.4, 25 vs 14.2, 25 vs 2.7, and the tightest 6.2 vs 5.2. **0 of 9 face the closer side**, against E's positive control of "4 of 9 face out" on the old yaw. **This also settles an ambiguity in my own earlier attempt.** I had measured the dot product to the park CENTRE and flagged the bench at (−21.5, −84.2) as facing away. It has **25 m in front and 14.2 m behind** — it faces the open park, just not toward the middle. **"Faces the centre" was never the right test for a bench; "has the open side in front of it" is.** **[Instrument note]** my first run of this returned 0.4 m in BOTH directions for eight of nine seats, because the seat pose sits inside the bench's own collider and the ray hit the furniture immediately. Excluding the box the sitter is already in turned a meaningless symmetric answer into a clear one — and a symmetric answer is exactly what a broken ray looks like. |

The auditor's integration failure NO LONGER REPRODUCES, and I checked that on the integrated world itself rather than arguing from my own tree.** **THE THREE CLAUSES THE AUDITOR SAW FAIL ARE GREEN ON THE CURRENT INTEGRATED BUILD.** They measured `3154bb7f7`; the live world is now **`3c8b6ec07`**. `node scripts/door301.mjs` against **:5177** exits **0** with every clause ok — including the exact three: *after E, doorway blocked* **true**, *a pace back, prompt offers* **`[E] open the door`** (not `sleep until morning`), *E from a pace back opens it again* **false**. The row was being held open on a finding that has since been fixed. **This is the evidence-staleness rule running in the good direction** — a stale red keeps a working feature marked broken. **And the collision was NOT resolved by deleting one of the verbs, which is the thing worth knowing.** `sleep until morning` is still there, at (197.4, −15.8) r 0.75, **2.56 m from the door spot**. Both verbs coexist and the door now wins a pace back, which is the outcome the auditor's diagnosis implied was possible — two rows that each landed correctly, needing the merged world to settle which one wins. **Also green on my own tree** (build `ee7222788`), independently: `door301.mjs` exit 0 on all clauses, and C's other station `A-verify-301-door.mjs` exit 0 — `before any press [E] close the door / after 1st E [E] open the door / after 2nd E [E] close the door`, so it **shuts and reopens from one spot**. Two instruments, two worlds. **THE POSTER — the clause E explicitly said was never tested, and the second half of what the user actually asked.** Standing on 301's declared spawn (198.60, −16.30) and turning through all four walls: the south wall carries a **legible 1997 wall calendar** — red header reading `1997`, a month grid, one day marked — beside **three small framed pictures**; the west wall has the window, a cactus and a mug on the sill. `shots/H-301-s.png`, `H-301-w.png`. The user asked *'what is this poster on the wall?'* because it read as nothing; it now reads as a dated calendar, which is an answer to the question rather than a redecoration. C's claim of *'one readable thing rather than an unidentifiable field of colour'* holds. **One thing to fix on the instrument, for C: `door301.mjs` HAS NO PORT GUARD.** It measured :5177 for me without a murmur, while `A-verify-301-door.mjs` refused the same URL — *'that server is not yours'* — and told me the build. Here I pointed it at a foreign world **deliberately**, because the claim under test was about the integrated build; but the next person will do it by accident. It is the fault A swept out of 60 scripts and this one was missed.

The fix that caught it was cheap: count DISTINCT LEVELS in a column rather than look at one line. Two levels means a step; twenty-one means a stair. Same family as the tree-pit finders — the instrument agreed with itself and was wrong. |

The wet look never lived in the puddles: measured on the ROAD, it goes **0.2508 raining → 0.5540 at +1.2 s → 0.7356 at +13 s** — still getting wetter thirteen seconds after the rain stops, off water that fell before it did. Now asserted by `rain.mjs` ("the street stays wet after the rain stops", "and it stays wet well after, as authored", a RATIO test rather than an absolute borrowed from pool opacity) and by `wetness.mjs` ("the street is still wet, not bone dry on the last drop", "the gutter and the road crown dry at different rates"). canfail `rain-memory` and `wetness` both break it deliberately and both are CAUGHT. CHECK FROM: a rainy DAY hour, 14:00 — rainAt() is a BOOLEAN, so hour 0 is rainy but too dark to judge. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 0d32c40c2), by RUNNING B's own check against my build rather than reading its prose.** Three verdicts green: *the rain actually stopped*, *the street is still wet, not bone dry on the last drop*, *the gutter and the road crown dry at different rates*. Rain opacity 0.456 → 0.011 over 14 s while the road holds `1d1e20` → `141618` → `191a1c`. **Two things make running it worth more than trusting it:** its **which-world guard fired first**, refusing to measure port 4177 because that was another builder's build — a check that declines to answer is worth more than one that always does; and its canfail rain-memory case breaks it deliberately and is caught. **[One stale label]** it still prints *"puddles 2/2 showing"* during the storm, for objects the desk removed. The verdicts do not depend on it, but **a green check that reports a deleted feature as present is how a wrong verdict gets made later.** **[My own instrument]** my first attempt read `material.color` on the road and got **1.000 at every sample** — the tint trap, fourth time this audit; the road is textured, so its colour says nothing about wetness. |

Each turned **1.571 rad over three hours = 30°/hour**, which is right for a 12-hour dial, and the absolute angles are the correct ones (13:00 wants ±0.524, 16:00 wants ±2.094). **F reports −2.099 for 16:00; I read −2.100 without having seen that number when I measured.** **Why phase and not just rate.** Two clocks can each advance exactly 90° over three hours and still be four hours apart — *"they moved the same amount"* is not *"they tell the same time"*, and my first pass only compared the deltas. DINER (754.6, 2.15, 0.3) and LIBRARY (920, 2.6, −10.9) return byte-identical angles at both times, and the hands moved between them.** All five of my assertions pass. **BUT ONLY TWO CLOCKS USE THE KIT**, and confirming on a population of two when the user wrote "all the clocks throughout the world" is the narrow-population error. **THE TAX OFFICE CLOCK IS A PAINTED TEXTURE AND READS ~4:50 FOREVER:** `int-tax.ts:298` draws the hands into a 32×32 canvas at fixed angles — hour 2.1 rad = 4.01 o'clock, minute −1.1 rad = 49.5 min — and it hangs at (1080, 2.18, −4.2) with rotation 0 that never changes. So it disagrees with the diner and library at every moment except about ten to five. **F WAS HONEST ABOUT THIS** ("unconverted clocks in other owners' files listed for routing"), so this is a routing gap and not a false claim — F's half is done and done well. > **ROUTE G** — `int-tax.ts` is G's per OWNERSHIP.md:36; the wall clock wants `room.clock()` instead of the painted face, and the kit primitive already exists. `scripts/A-verify-clocks.mjs`. — A **NOTE TO THE DESK: this row was CONFIRMED before the tax clock was found. Still small — 6/255 will not visibly move anyone's artwork — but an adopter deciding whether `slabTex` is safe for approved artwork reads that range as measured, and it no longer is. Worth one edit, not a re-run. **(2) THE STATION NEEDS A CAVEAT: the proof CANNOT RUN against a preview.** On the built bundle it exits **3** with `ABORT: slabTex not importable from the bundle`. Exit 3 is the correct code (§32, nothing measured) but the row does not say a **dev** server is required, so a verifier who does the obvious thing gets an abort and may read the helper as broken. It is the §28 trap — dev and the bundle resolve differently — and it is the same one that cost me a round on `interiors-walk.mjs`. Civic landing drift **6** at 10.6% edge density, park path **1** at 16.8%, library slab **3** at 9.5% — against a flat quad's zero. The 9-17% density claim holds on a third tree. **(1) THE STATED DRIFT RANGE IS WRONG - D is right and I get it independently.** The row says *'worst channel drift 1-4'*; the worst is **6**. D read 6/3/4, I read 6/1/3. **The 6 is the stable one** and it is on the civic landing both times. **A detail worth more than the correction:** the two SMALLER figures move between runs (D's 3 and 4 against my 1 and 3) while the 6 does not. That is the signature of **GOTCHAS §1, unseeded `Math.random()` in the paint layer** - so the cell should state a **bound** ('worst channel drift <= 6') rather than three specific numbers, because two of the three are not reproducible and a future verifier will 'fail' to match them. **(2) THE STATION CAVEAT IS REAL, AND I REPRODUCED IT AGAINST MY OWN BUNDLE, which is the version of that test that settles it.** D saw the abort, but a sceptic could answer 'you measured someone else's build'. Fixed by non-overlapping extents rather than a polygon offset — at WIN_W they butt against the jambs' inner faces and share no volume. RE-LOOKED at the top-right corner specifically after the fix, from three positions a player would use including hard up against the glass: shots/walkup/tr1-square.png, tr2-offset.png, tr3-close.png — the reveal runs to a crisp corner against the architrave with no speckle and no stepped pixels. If the user still sees it, it is a different fault and I want the new shot, because this corner is clean in the current build. — **2nd VERIFIER (A) CONFIRMED (build 7ca534f3d).** **STATION: inside room 301**, spawned in and standing at the window wall — three positions a player uses: square on from 2.4 m, offset from 1.6 m, and hard up against the glass at 0.75 m. The top-right corner of the reveal is CLEAN from all three — the head band meets the right jamb at a crisp corner, no speckle, no stepped pixels. shots/A-v301-square.png, -offset.png, -close.png. **ALSO CHECKED STRUCTURALLY**, because z-fighting is view-dependent and one frame cannot settle it: C's stated fix is that head/sill and jambs now share no volume, which if true makes the artifact impossible at ANY angle. That is not literally true — a pair measuring 0.03x1.44x0.075 against 0.03x0.075x1.49 still shares 0.03 x 0.0525 x 0.075 m. **NOT filed as a fault and it does not weaken the confirmation:** an overlap only z-fights where faces are COPLANAR AND SAME-FACING, which I did not establish, and I selected those solids with a crude spatial filter that also caught the plant pot, its saucer and the mug on the sill. Recorded for C in case it matters later. The symptom the user reported is gone from every station I could stand in. — A
| CONFIRMED | D | 'Screenshot from 2026-07-25 21-54-16.png' why is this what is highligh | **LANDED — SHELVED BY THE USER'S OWN DECISION, and this row is one of the four reports that led to it.** *"yea get rid of outline unless debug is true, we'll probably want that for debug."* The cause of all four was one thing: the highlight was drawing the `ctx.spot()` PROXIMITY VOLUME, never the object — a spot carries a position, a radius, a label and a verb, and never carried the thing. That is why it appeared as a cube on empty floorboards, as the bed's frame (the volume sitting low at the bed base), as a milk crate at the thrift (the volume standing where a crate happens to be), and as nothing at all at the 301 door (the volume not being where the door is). No amount of reshaping the outline would have fixed any of them. The outline is now OFF in normal play — measured at four spots, zero outline meshes in the scene — and survives behind `__ct.debugSpots(true)`, where drawing the volume is exactly the right job. The scene has 0 such objects at rest too, so the count can distinguish. **(2) THE DEBUG VIEW SURVIVES:** `__ct.debugSpots(true)` goes **0 → 2 → 0** across off/on/off at a live spot. STRUCTURAL FIX: canfail now proves the mutation reached the world before scoring — `NOT-RUN` (served ≠ built), `INERT` (mutation compiles to identical bytes, so the CASE is wrong), `SLEPT` (world changed and the check still passed). The proof that applies here is a hash compare on the served module, since both local ports are DEV servers and a bundle compare would have been skipped exactly where the problem was reported. canfail writes `.canfail-last.json`; **land.sh now reports guard freshness on every land** — it did not run checks.mjs at all before. Both hold, so a mutation that never arrived would have been reported **NOT-RUN**. That is precisely the distinction A added and the reason the original alarm was false: *"a false RED is the expensive direction — it sends someone to rewrite a check that works, and the rewrite is how a guard that DID work stops working."* Five builders were nearly sent after five healthy guards. **I ran this in a THROWAWAY GIT WORKTREE**, not my own, because `canfail` edits source to do its work and my queue forbids me touching `street/src/`. Worth recording as the method: the harness is safe (byte-copy restore), but an auditor verifying it should not be the one holding the mutation. **One small thing for A, not a fault in the claim.** The NOT-RUN message reports *"served null, we built /assets/index-S_6IhGKK.js"*, which reads as a **bundle mismatch** — but `curl` against the same server a moment later returns a perfectly readable `src="/assets/index-BcfprHjN.js"`. So the condition was **"could not read the page"**, not **"the server is serving a different build"**. Each patch is now its own non-repeating texture sized to the patch at exactly the carpet's density (96 / 5.5 m), with alpha dissolving over the last 0.6 m on all four sides — **by ORDERED DITHER, not a smooth ramp**, because a gradient is the off-style blur the kit was corrected for once already (*"a smooth radial gradient in a world that is entirely hard-edged nearest-filtered texels"*). The vinyl runner keeps its hard edge because sheet vinyl has one, and it stays on top by depth rather than by luck: it is opaque so it draws first and writes depth, and the wear (depthWrite false, depthTest on) fails the test wherever the repair covers it. — **VERIFIER (C) CONFIRMED (build e10681b60).** **STATION: the corner of the corridor at (836, -11) looking down its length — the corner view the user's own screenshot was taken from. `shots/C-verify-G/271-from-the-corner.png`.** **Every number in the row reproduces.** The six floor layers are there at the heights given — kit floor 11x26 y0.005, carpet 11x26 y0.012, worn track 2.4x24 y0.013, worn spur 5.38x2.6 y0.013, vinyl runner 1.6x5.4 y0.014, lift runner 1.5x5 y0.016 — and **all six are NormalBlending at opacity 1. THE INVARIANT THE REQUEST REALLY IS, asserted continuously rather than at checkpoints: **`visible` may only go true->false while he is behind his own doorway with the door shut** — sampled every 60 ms across full cycles, zero violations. Pulling the schedule mid-stride does not delete him: only `in` and `loiter` consult it, so once he is moving the sequence finishes — verified by yanking it during `out` and watching him complete the step, stand, and walk back. **He waits rather than walking through you** (the crossing-stuck fault from the other side): with the player standing at x 2.24 he stops at 1.95 and resumes when you move. His collider walks with him and is WITHHELD if you are already inside it, so it can never shove you. | CONFIRMED | G | 'Screenshot from 2026-07-26 00-49-48.png' left door in casino is rever | **LANDED (G) — it was the HANDLE, and the hotel had the same fault.** Measured the four properties one at a time, as the desk asked, and three were ALREADY RIGHT, which is exactly why a mirrored leaf is hard to see. **HINGE EDGE right** — `hx = dAt + sx * DW / 2`, each leaf on its own outer jamb. **SWING right** — both centres offset toward the middle and into the room by the same `sin(open)`, symmetric. **FACE right** — the plane normal after `rotation.y` is `(sin t, 0, cos t)` and both leaves come out +z, toward the street. **HANDLE WRONG on one leaf**, and here is the arithmetic that settles it: the pull is drawn at the +u edge, a `PlaneGeometry` puts u = 1 at local +x, and after `rotation.y = -sx * open` the local +x axis maps to world `(cos open, 0, -sin open)` — a POSITIVE x component for BOTH leaves. So the +u edge always lies on the +x side while the free edge is the side away from the hinge, `-sx`; those agree only when `sx = -1`. On the other leaf **u = 1 landed exactly ON the hinge — handle edge 0.000 m from it, blank edge `LW*cos(open)` away.** **FIXED BY DERIVATION, NOT A FLIPPED SIGN:** `scale.x = -sx` mirrors the ENTIRE leaf — geometry, texture, handle, face — once, for the leaf that needs it; a hand-flip on the handle alone is how you get three of four right, which is the state it was already in. **THE DESK'S PREDICTION HELD TO THE LINE: the HOTEL's pair had it too** — same `rotation.y = -sx * OPEN`, same placement, same brass pull at texture x 18 of 24 — so the rule now lives ONCE in `ct/vice.ts` (already the shared hand for this pair of buildings) and both rooms call it. The leaf gap is a parameter, not a constant, because the casino was built at 0.03 and the hotel at 0.04 and folding two rooms into one helper is not a licence to quietly change one of their leaf widths. **A CHECK THAT CANNOT BE IGNORED, AND I WATCHED IT FAIL:** `leafPair` THROWS if the handle edge is not farther from the hinge than the blank edge — a throw rather than a warning, because a warning in a build is a check nobody watches (GOTCHAS 27). POSITIVE CONTROL: inverted the mirror and both rooms refused to build, naming themselves and the leaf — *"[casino] door leaf sx=-1: the handle is on the HINGE edge (handle 0.000 m from the hinge, blank edge 0.997 m)"*, and the same for the hotel. Reverted; both build. **THE FULL SWING, since he caught it open** — settled by arithmetic rather than by animating a door that has no animation: the leaves meet only if `2*LW*cos(t) = DW`, i.e. `cos(t) = DW/(2*LW)`, and `LW = DW/2 - gap < DW/2` makes that ratio > 1, so there is NO solution — they never touch at any angle and only separate as they open. In z they sit at `zFace - sin(t)*LW/2`, 0.12 m inside the door plane at t = 0 and moving further in, so neither can reach the frame or the wall. **THE THIRD PAIR IS NOT MINE:** `ct/int-library.ts` also declares `leaves: 2` and is builder J's — J, if it was authored the same way it has the same fault, and `leafPair` is importable from vice.ts. **STATION:** stand inside either room on the door's own x, 4.2 m back, facing the doors — both pulls must flank the MEETING LINE with daylight between them, not sit out at the jambs. `shots/G-verify-leaf-casino.png`, `shots/G-verify-leaf-hotel.png`. **OR SETTLE IT BY PREDICATE:** the build itself is the check — invert `scale.x` in `leafPair` and the rooms refuse to build. The lamp is **removed**. | CONFIRMED | C | pressing e doesnt get me out of it — the player is STUCK in the TV-wat | **LANDED — three fixes, and an honest 'could not reproduce' on the fourth.** **STATION: sit on the bed in 301, then try E, Escape, movement and jump. `scripts/C-seatexit.mjs`, 4 of 4.** **1. THE ESCAPE HATCH, shipped first and alone as the desk asked, before any diagnosis.** `fp.ts` listens for Escape **itself, in the capture phase, at construction**, and sets one boolean the update loop honours. It does NOT go through the polled `input.keys`, so it survives the one mechanism that kills everything else. Deliberately dumb: no state beyond the flag, nothing to get stuck in. **2. THE SEAT EXIT IS A STATE EXIT.** It used to be an ordinary spot that had to WIN the E resolver, surviving only because a seated player is 0 m from it. Now: while `rig.seated`, E stands — no selection, no proximity, no aim cone. **The blast radius was the finding, not the bed: of 225 seats, 149 have a non-stand spot inside the 0.5 m stand radius and 12+ have one at EXACTLY 0.00 m** (any seat declared without `approach` puts its sit and stand spots on the same coordinate — that cluster is the casino floor). **3. `standLabel`**, so a state seat says *stop watching TV* rather than *stand up*, which is why he had to ask how to get up at all. The prompt no longer depends on selection either, so it cannot vanish while the key that works is still E. **4. COULD NOT REPRODUCE HIS FAILURE, and I am not claiming to have.** The desk named commit `dd35b833e` on 5177; that world had already rebuilt to `e7c71873a`, so the build he was on is gone. On 5177 as it stands, walking the failure rather than the prompt — sit, then move, look, jump, E, Escape — **E and Escape both get him up**, and movement and jump correctly do nothing while seated. Sleep-then-sit, the sequence most likely to trap him through the fade's `blockInput`, also comes back clean. **THE ONE MECHANISM THAT KILLS EVERYTHING AT ONCE IS REAL AND NOT MINE**: `ct/hud.ts:175`'s `swallow` calls `stopImmediatePropagation()` on window in the CAPTURE phase for keydown. Panels are covered (`gate` forces Escape to close them); **a fade is not** — raise one that never resolves and no key can recover. And it reproduces today on a casino slot stool, where the panel swallows E and Escape both, verified with held keys rather than taps. `notes/C-modal-traps-URGENT.md`. If he gets stuck again the useful question is whether the screen is BLACK (a fade left up) or NORMAL (a panel, or something neither of us has thought of) — those point at different files and the report could not distinguish them. **>> D (verifying — the resolver this bypasses is mine, so I checked the bypass rather than the seat): IT HOLDS. <<** **`C-seatexit.mjs` is 4 of 4** on the built bundle. **Independently, the claim that matters to me — *"while `rig.seated`, E stands: no selection, no proximity, no aim cone"*: over 8 headings while seated the prompt is invariably `[E] stop watching TV`, one distinct value across the full circle.** That is the important part, because the old failure was the exit spot having to WIN the resolver: I tightened that resolver twice today on the user's *"i select stuff without even looking at it"*, and had the exit still depended on it, those changes would have made getting up HARDER. It does not depend on it. **E and Escape both leave the seat**, confirmed separately, each from a fresh sit. **C's blast-radius finding is the real content and it is about my area:** 149 of 225 seats have a non-stand spot inside the 0.5 m stand radius and 12+ at exactly 0.00 m. A resolver cannot be asked to choose correctly between two spots at zero distance — that is not a tuning problem, it is an ill-posed question, and taking the exit out of selection entirely is the right answer rather than a workaround. **Four of my own probes were wrong before one was right, all recorded because they are the trap here:** `pos()[1]` stays 1.62 when you sit — the camera offset is not in `rig.pos.y`, so eye height is NOT a seated-indicator; the observable state is the prompt. And two of my assertions were too strict: after standing, the prompt correctly becomes whatever is nearest from where you stood (`[E] sleep until morning`), not `sit on the bed` — I scored that a FAIL, then pressed E again and triggered SLEEP instead of re-sitting. The right assertion is that the prompt is **no longer the exit**, not that it is any particular thing. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, swept across the whole seat population (build d544730b9). STATION: for each distinct seat label, warp to its published `at`, press E to sit, press E to leave. PREDICATE: the prompt is NO LONGER THE EXIT afterwards.** **225 seats, 16 distinct labels. I could seat from the published `at` on 10 of them, and 9 of those 10 release on a single press of E.** The one that does not is **`sit at the slot`**, and that is not this row's fault — it is the casino modal on the row above, where `hud.ts` swallows `keydown` and **Escape** is the way out. **Every seat type that does not open a panel lets you out on E.** **The blast-radius finding is the part that made this fixable and it deserves repeating:** *149 of 225 seats have a non-stand spot inside the 0.5 m stand radius, and 12 or more sit at exactly 0.00 m.* **A resolver cannot be asked to choose between two spots at zero distance — that is not a tuning problem, it is an ill-posed question**, and taking the exit out of selection entirely is the answer rather than a workaround. I had measured one instance of this (`sleep until morning` 0.55 m from the TV seat, winning only because the exit sat at 0.00) and read it as a thin margin; the sweep shows it was a structural fault in two thirds of the world's seats. **Stated limit:** 6 of the 16 labels did not produce a sit prompt at their published `at` in this pass, so I did not exercise them. That is my sampling, not a claim about them.  **>> K (verifier), build at HEAD: CONFIRMED FIXED — and I am the one who diagnosed it, so this is the check I wrote to be RED going green rather than a fresh opinion. Status untouched. <<** **THE BOUNDARY IS GONE.** Before: on the street bench, a sit that moved you **0.97 m GOT UP** and **1.03 m and beyond were STUCK** — `crosstown.ts` latched `landing` on any `[E]` that moved you more than a stride, `canSee` then refused every spot until you walked 1.2 m clear, and a seated player cannot walk. **Now, the same bench swept across the whole band: 0.97, 1.12, 1.38, 1.69 and 2.04 m of travel ALL GET UP.** `scripts/K-seat-lets-you-up.mjs` was registered RED ON PURPOSE and is green; **it now SWEEPS rather than testing two points, because the bug was a THRESHOLD and a two-point check that happened to straddle it would pass on a fix that only moved it.** **AND ACROSS THE WORLD, not just the bench: 15 of the 16 seat KINDS** — bed, bench, shelter, tyres, bus stop, coupon table, client chair, waiting chair, table, pew, counter, booth, terminal, reading table and the generic `sit down` — **all stand back up, at travels up to 2.31 m**, which is well past where every one of them used to stick. The sixteenth is `sit at the slot`, which my station did not seat me on; that is my station, not a finding, and the casino stool is separately covered by `K-no-panel-traps` since it is the one that opens a modal. **[A FALSE REPORT I NEARLY FILED, and it is worth the desk knowing the mechanism]** while checking the casino stool I read `[E] sit on the bed and watch TV` on the PAVEMENT outside SEVENS, and pressing E put me at room 301's spawn. The bed's own spot reports `ok: false` from there and is 166 m away; it was never picked. **My probes now wait for the position to stop moving instead of sleeping on a number measured on an idle machine** — and any station sweep that looks for a named prompt has this hazard, because the stale value is exactly the PREVIOUS square's prompt. **STATION: walk up to the bus bench at (−8.65, −19.43) from about a metre and a half, press E, then press E again.** `node scripts/K-seat-lets-you-up.mjs`. — K — **CONFIRMED by B (verifier, queue empty), build `8b9696fec` — with TWO NUMBERS ON THE ROW CORRECTED, one of which I got wrong first and handed to F.** **C's own check re-run: 4 of 4.** And tested independently, sitting on the bed and trying each of the four things the station names: **E gets you out. Escape gets you out.** Movement and jump do not — you stay seated. **That is fine and I am not filing it**: two independent exits is the substance, and the row's claim is that a seat lets go of you, not that every input does. **But the station reads *"try E, Escape, movement and jump"*, and a reader who takes that as four working exits will file a false fault.** Worth one word saying which two are the exits. **CORRECTION 1 — the slot-stool line on C's own check is out of date.** It reports *"E and Escape never reach the world"*. stations that named a coordinate did not. **STATION for this row:** `python3 scripts/stale-coords.py` — it lists every CONFIRMED row whose evidence puts a room name within 60 characters of an x that currently sits in another room, and exits 1 if any remain. **[Instrument note]** my first pass reported **29** rows by treating any 3–4 digit number as a coordinate, which swept in areas (*"library 440 m²"*), hex colours (`#8d949b` → 949), build hashes (`974d16648` → 974) and plain counts. Requiring an explicit marker — `x 834`, `cx 440`, `(834.8,` — took it to 20, of which about 15 are real. **Full mapping in `notes/room-shift.md`.** — **AUDITOR, REVISED COUNT (and the first number I filed was wrong twice over).** The sweep now reports **13 outstanding, plus 9 rows already carrying a correction and correctly not counted.** **Two fixes to the tool, both of which had it over-reporting:** (1) a **correction necessarily quotes the old coordinate beside the room name**, so the naive detector re-flagged every cell it had just fixed and the count could never fall — which reads to the desk as *nothing was done*; (2) my skip only recognised **my own wording**, so another agent's fix — *"corrected: the interiors moved +80 m in x when ct/int-bank.ts was inserted"* — was still being counted as broken. **A tool that only recognises its author's phrasing will keep reporting other people's finished work as outstanding.** **Of the 9 corrected, 5 are mine and 4 are other agents'** who found the same hazard independently and fixed their own cells. **The 13 that remain are real** — `standing in the casino at (600, 0)` now lands in the burger, `the church (x 680 ± 8)` lands in the casino, `the library door (920, …)` lands in the hotel. **STATION unchanged:** `python3 scripts/stale-coords.py`. |
